Even when my children come over to sit with me, or the sun is shining right on my garden, I just feel completely flat inside. It is like the part of me that could be glad, or even just feel comforted by those things, just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods like this before in your life, where you felt this kind of flatness and inability to enjoy things for weeks or months at a time?\n\nPatient: No, never like this. I mean, I've had sad days, of course, just like anyone my age. But this... this complete emptiness, where nothing touches me at all? No, this is entirely new. I've never felt this way before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life, even a shorter one, where you felt depressed or down for at least a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I really haven't. Just the normal ups and downs, you know? I've never been stuck feeling down or depressed for a couple of weeks straight. That's why this whole thing is just so strange to me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this complete emptiness and flatness where nothing brings you joy or comfort?\n\nPatient: It's been about two weeks now. It started right after... well, right after my husband collapsed. The paramedics were working on him right there in our kitchen, and ever since that day, I've just felt this way.\n\nDoctor: When your husband collapsed in the kitchen, what happened after the paramedics arrived? Is he still alive, or did he pass away?\n\nPatient: He... he passed away. \n\nI remember him falling, and I can clearly see the men working on him in the kitchen. But... I cannot remember what I said on the phone to get them there. I don't even know how the paramedics got through the front door. No matter how hard I try to think back on it, those parts are just completely gone.\n\nDoctor: When you first learned about your husband's death or right after it happened, did you feel disconnected from your body, or like you were watching everything happen from outside yourself—before you started feeling this flatness and numbness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Um... sometimes I walk through my own kitchen and it just looks like a room in someone else’s house. It feels like I’m moving around in a dream, like I'm not really there.\n\nDoctor: When you say the flatness and emptiness started right after your husband collapsed—did these feelings begin on that same day, or did they start a few days later?\n\nPatient: It started that very same day. Yes. Like I said, this has all been going on for about two weeks now... just ever since he collapsed and the paramedics were working on him in our kitchen. The emptiness just started right then.\n\nDoctor: During those moments when the paramedics were working on your husband in the kitchen, do you remember feeling like you were watching it all happen from far away, or like it wasn't really happening to you—even though you were right there?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I remember being right there in the room. I wasn't watching from far away or anything like that. I just... I was just there, watching them work on him.\n\nDoctor: In those first few hours or days right after your husband collapsed, did you experience moments where you felt numb or detached from what was happening around you—like you were observing everything but not really feeling connected to it, even though you were physically present?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um... I know I said the emptiness started right away, but I didn't feel detached like that. I wasn't just observing it. I was right there, and I knew exactly what was happening... I just felt completely flat.\n\nDoctor: When the paramedics were working on your husband in the kitchen, did you feel like your own body or your surroundings seemed unreal or strange to you—like things weren't quite real, even though you knew they were happening?\n\nPatient: Well, it didn't feel like that right when the paramedics were there... but lately, yes, things have felt strange. Sometimes I'll just be walking through my own kitchen, and it looks... different. Like it's a room in someone else's house. It feels like I'm moving around in a dream, like I'm not really there. It's just a very odd feeling.\n\nDoctor: When exactly did you first start noticing that things felt unreal or dream-like, and that your kitchen looked like someone else's house? Was that feeling there right from the moment the paramedics arrived, or did it develop gradually over the days and weeks after your husband's death?\n\nPatient: Well... it's been going on for about two weeks. Basically ever since my husband collapsed and the paramedics worked on him in our kitchen. Like I said, I didn't feel it the exact minute they were in there, but it started right after that day. So it's been about two weeks now of things just... feeling strange like that.\n\nDoctor: When you're going about your day—like making breakfast or watching television—do you feel that dream-like, unreal sensation most of the time, or does it mainly happen when you're in the kitchen or thinking about what happened to your husband?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. Um... I haven't really kept track of exactly when it happens. I know it happens in the kitchen, like I mentioned, but I couldn't tell you if it's going on most of the day or just when my mind goes back to what happened. I just haven't noticed a specific pattern to it.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something completely unrelated to what happened—like cooking dinner or talking on the phone with a friend—do you still get that dream-like, unreal feeling, or does it mainly come back when you're thinking about your husband or in the kitchen?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, I really don't know for sure. I haven't paid attention to whether I'm on the phone or doing something else when it happens. I'm sorry, I just haven't kept track of it that closely. It just sort of comes over me sometimes, but I couldn't tell you exactly what I'm doing when it starts.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It started right after... well, right after my husband collapsed. Is he still alive, or did he pass away?\n\nPatient: He... he passed away. \n\nI remember him falling, and I can clearly see the men working on him in the kitchen. But... It feels like I’m moving around in a dream, like I'm not really there.\n\nDoctor: When you say the flatness and emptiness started right after your husband collapsed—did these feelings begin on that same day, or did they start a few days later?\n\nPatient: It started that very same day. No matter how hard I try to think back on it, those parts are just completely gone.\n\nDoctor: When you first learned about your husband's death or right after it happened, did you feel disconnected from your body, or like you were watching everything happen from outside yourself—before you started feeling this flatness and numbness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Um... sometimes I walk through my own kitchen and it just looks like a room in someone else’s house. It feels like I’m moving around in a dream, like I'm not really there.\n\nDoctor: When you say the flatness and emptiness started right after your husband collapsed—did these feelings begin on that same day, or did they start a few days later?\n\nPatient: It started that very same day. Yes. Like I said, this has all been going on for about two weeks now... just ever since he collapsed and the paramedics were working on him in our kitchen. The emptiness just started right then.\n\nDoctor: During those moments when the paramedics were working on your husband in the kitchen, do you remember feeling like you were watching it all happen from far away, or like it wasn't really happening to you—even though you were right there?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I remember being right there in the room. I wasn't watching from far away or anything like that. I just... I was just there, watching them work on him.\n\nDoctor: In those first few hours or days right after your husband collapsed, did you experience moments where you felt numb or detached from what was happening around you—like you were observing everything but not really feeling connected to it, even though you were physically present?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um... I know I said the emptiness started right away, but I didn't feel detached like that. I wasn't just observing it. I was right there, and I knew exactly what was happening... I just felt completely flat.\n\nDoctor: When the paramedics were working on your husband in the kitchen, did you feel like your own body or your surroundings seemed unreal or strange to you—like things weren't quite real, even though you knew they were happening?\n\nPatient: Well, it didn't feel like that right when the paramedics were there... but lately, yes, things have felt strange. Sometimes I'll just be walking through my own kitchen, and it looks... different. Like it's a room in someone else's house. It feels like I'm moving around in a dream, like I'm not really there. It's just a very odd feeling.\n\nDoctor: When exactly did you first start noticing that things felt unreal or dream-like, and that your kitchen looked like someone else's house? Was that feeling there right from the moment the paramedics arrived, or did it develop gradually over the days and weeks after your husband's death?\n\nPatient: Well... it's been going on for about two weeks. Basically ever since my husband collapsed and the paramedics worked on him in our kitchen. Like I said, I didn't feel it the exact minute they were in there, but it started right after that day. So it's been about two weeks now of things just... feeling strange like that.\n\nDoctor: When you're going about your day—like making breakfast or watching television—do you feel that dream-like, unreal sensation most of the time, or does it mainly happen when you're in the kitchen or thinking about what happened to your husband?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. Um... I haven't really kept track of exactly when it happens. I know it happens in the kitchen, like I mentioned, but I couldn't tell you if it's going on most of the day or just when my mind goes back to what happened. I just haven't noticed a specific pattern to it.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something completely unrelated to what happened—like cooking dinner or talking on the phone with a friend—do you still get that dream-like, unreal feeling, or does it mainly come back when you're thinking about your husband or in the kitchen?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, I really don't know for sure. I haven't paid attention to whether I'm on the phone or doing something else when it happens. I'm sorry, I just haven't kept track of it that closely. It just sort of comes over me sometimes, but I couldn't tell you exactly what I'm doing when it starts.\n\nDoctor: Right now, in this moment, sitting here talking with me in my office—do you feel that same dream-like, unreal sensation, or does that feeling mainly happen when you're at home or when your mind goes back to what happened with your husband?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel it right now. Sitting here with you, things seem... normal, I guess. It's not happening at this exact moment. Like I said before, I just haven't figured out exactly when or why it starts up.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that most of your day feels dream-like and unreal, or is it more like you have moments or episodes where it happens and then it passes?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say if it's most of the day or just in moments. I just haven't been keeping track of it like that. It happens, but I don't know how long it lasts or how much of the day it takes up. I'm sorry, I just don't have a good sense of the time when it comes to that feeling.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, before you've had time to think about what happened to your husband, do you already feel that dream-like, unreal sensation—or does it only start once you begin thinking about him or going about your day?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I've been saying, I just haven't been paying that much attention to when it starts or stops. I wake up, and... I don't know if it's there right away or if it comes on later. I'm sorry, I just really haven't noticed if it happens first thing in the morning or not.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

The key distinction is whether symptoms persist throughout the day independent of trauma cues, or whether they're triggered by specific reminders of the husband's death.",

I was just there, watching them work on him.\n\nDoctor: In those first few hours or days right after your husband collapsed, did you experience moments where you felt numb or detached from what was happening around you—like you were observing everything but not really feeling connected to it, even though you were physically present?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um... I know I said the emptiness started right away, but I didn't feel detached like that. I wasn't just observing it. I was right there, and I knew exactly what was happening... I just felt completely flat.\n\nDoctor: When the paramedics were working on your husband in the kitchen, did you feel like your own body or your surroundings seemed unreal or strange to you—like things weren't quite real, even though you knew they were happening?\n\nPatient: Well, it didn't feel like that right when the paramedics were there... but lately, yes, things have felt strange. Sometimes I'll just be walking through my own kitchen, and it looks... different. Like it's a room in someone else's house. It feels like I'm moving around in a dream, like I'm not really there. It's just a very odd feeling.\n\nDoctor: When exactly did you first start noticing that things felt unreal or dream-like, and that your kitchen looked like someone else's house? Was that feeling there right from the moment the paramedics arrived, or did it develop gradually over the days and weeks after your husband's death?\n\nPatient: Well... it's been going on for about two weeks. Basically ever since my husband collapsed and the paramedics worked on him in our kitchen. Like I said, I didn't feel it the exact minute they were in there, but it started right after that day. So it's been about two weeks now of things just... feeling strange like that.\n\nDoctor: When you're going about your day—like making breakfast or watching television—do you feel that dream-like, unreal sensation most of the time, or does it mainly happen when you're in the kitchen or thinking about what happened to your husband?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. Um... I haven't really kept track of exactly when it happens. I know it happens in the kitchen, like I mentioned, but I couldn't tell you if it's going on most of the day or just when my mind goes back to what happened. I just haven't noticed a specific pattern to it.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something completely unrelated to what happened—like cooking dinner or talking on the phone with a friend—do you still get that dream-like, unreal feeling, or does it mainly come back when you're thinking about your husband or in the kitchen?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, I really don't know for sure. I haven't paid attention to whether I'm on the phone or doing something else when it happens. I'm sorry, I just haven't kept track of it that closely. It just sort of comes over me sometimes, but I couldn't tell you exactly what I'm doing when it starts.\n\nDoctor: Right now, in this moment, sitting here talking with me in my office—do you feel that same dream-like, unreal sensation, or does that feeling mainly happen when you're at home or when your mind goes back to what happened with your husband?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel it right now. Sitting here with you, things seem... normal, I guess. It's not happening at this exact moment. Like I said before, I just haven't figured out exactly when or why it starts up.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that most of your day feels dream-like and unreal, or is it more like you have moments or episodes where it happens and then it passes?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say if it's most of the day or just in moments. I just haven't been keeping track of it like that. It happens, but I don't know how long it lasts or how much of the day it takes up. I'm sorry, I just don't have a good sense of the time when it comes to that feeling.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, before you've had time to think about what happened to your husband, do you already feel that dream-like, unreal sensation—or does it only start once you begin thinking about him or going about your day?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I've been saying, I just haven't been paying that much attention to when it starts or stops. I wake up, and... I don't know if it's there right away or if it comes on later. I'm sorry, I just really haven't noticed if it happens first thing in the morning or not.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband passed away two weeks ago, have there been any full days where you did NOT feel that dream-like, unreal sensation at all—or has it been happening every single day?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I... I just haven't been keeping track of days like that. The last two weeks are just a blur, honestly. I don't know if there was a day where it didn't happen at all, or if it's been every day. I'm sorry, I just don't know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I was just there, watching them work on him.\n\nDoctor: In those first few hours or days right after your husband collapsed, did you experience moments where you felt numb or detached from what was happening around you—like you were observing everything but not really feeling connected to it, even though you were physically present?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um... I know I said the emptiness started right away, but I didn't feel detached like that. I wasn't just observing it. I was right there, and I knew exactly what was happening... I just felt completely flat.\n\nDoctor: When the paramedics were working on your husband in the kitchen, did you feel like your own body or your surroundings seemed unreal or strange to you—like things weren't quite real, even though you knew they were happening?\n\nPatient: Well, it didn't feel like that right when the paramedics were there... but lately, yes, things have felt strange. Sometimes I'll just be walking through my own kitchen, and it looks... different. Like it's a room in someone else's house. It feels like I'm moving around in a dream, like I'm not really there. It's just a very odd feeling.\n\nDoctor: When exactly did you first start noticing that things felt unreal or dream-like, and that your kitchen looked like someone else's house? Was that feeling there right from the moment the paramedics arrived, or did it develop gradually over the days and weeks after your husband's death?\n\nPatient: Well... it's been going on for about two weeks. Basically ever since my husband collapsed and the paramedics worked on him in our kitchen. Like I said, I didn't feel it the exact minute they were in there, but it started right after that day. So it's been about two weeks now of things just... feeling strange like that.\n\nDoctor: When you're going about your day—like making breakfast or watching television—do you feel that dream-like, unreal sensation most of the time, or does it mainly happen when you're in the kitchen or thinking about what happened to your husband?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. Um... I haven't really kept track of exactly when it happens. I know it happens in the kitchen, like I mentioned, but I couldn't tell you if it's going on most of the day or just when my mind goes back to what happened. I just haven't noticed a specific pattern to it.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something completely unrelated to what happened—like cooking dinner or talking on the phone with a friend—do you still get that dream-like, unreal feeling, or does it mainly come back when you're thinking about your husband or in the kitchen?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, I really don't know for sure. I haven't paid attention to whether I'm on the phone or doing something else when it happens. I'm sorry, I just haven't kept track of it that closely. It just sort of comes over me sometimes, but I couldn't tell you exactly what I'm doing when it starts.\n\nDoctor: Right now, in this moment, sitting here talking with me in my office—do you feel that same dream-like, unreal sensation, or does that feeling mainly happen when you're at home or when your mind goes back to what happened with your husband?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel it right now. Sitting here with you, things seem... normal, I guess. It's not happening at this exact moment. Like I said before, I just haven't figured out exactly when or why it starts up.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that most of your day feels dream-like and unreal, or is it more like you have moments or episodes where it happens and then it passes?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say if it's most of the day or just in moments. I just haven't been keeping track of it like that. It happens, but I don't know how long it lasts or how much of the day it takes up. I'm sorry, I just don't have a good sense of the time when it comes to that feeling.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, before you've had time to think about what happened to your husband, do you already feel that dream-like, unreal sensation—or does it only start once you begin thinking about him or going about your day?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I've been saying, I just haven't been paying that much attention to when it starts or stops. I wake up, and... I don't know if it's there right away or if it comes on later. I'm sorry, I just really haven't noticed if it happens first thing in the morning or not.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband passed away two weeks ago, have there been any full days where you did NOT feel that dream-like, unreal sensation at all—or has it been happening every single day?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I... I just haven't been keeping track of days like that. The last two weeks are just a blur, honestly. I don't know if there was a day where it didn't happen at all, or if it's been every day. I'm sorry, I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that has nothing to do with your husband—like eating a meal, or taking a shower, or getting dressed—do you feel that dream-like, unreal sensation during those activities, or does it mainly happen when something reminds you of what happened?\n\nPatient: It happens during regular things, too. Like... sometimes I'll just be walking through my own kitchen, and it suddenly looks like a room in someone else's house. It doesn't even feel like mine. I just feel like I'm moving around in a dream and I'm not really there. So, yes, it happens even when I'm just doing normal things around the house.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No matter how hard I try to think back on it, those parts are just completely gone.\n\nDoctor: When you first learned about your husband's death or right after it happened, did you feel disconnected from your body, or like you were watching everything happen from outside yourself—before you started feeling this flatness and numbness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Um... sometimes I walk through my own kitchen and it just looks like a room in someone else’s house. It feels like I’m moving around in a dream, like I'm not really there.\n\nDoctor: When you say the flatness and emptiness started right after your husband collapsed—did these feelings begin on that same day, or did they start a few days later?\n\nPatient: It started that very same day. Yes. Like I said, this has all been going on for about two weeks now... just ever since he collapsed and the paramedics were working on him in our kitchen. The emptiness just started right then.\n\nDoctor: During those moments when the paramedics were working on your husband in the kitchen, do you remember feeling like you were watching it all happen from far away, or like it wasn't really happening to you—even though you were right there?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I remember being right there in the room. I wasn't watching from far away or anything like that. I just... I was just there, watching them work on him.\n\nDoctor: In those first few hours or days right after your husband collapsed, did you experience moments where you felt numb or detached from what was happening around you—like you were observing everything but not really feeling connected to it, even though you were physically present?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um... I know I said the emptiness started right away, but I didn't feel detached like that. I wasn't just observing it. I was right there, and I knew exactly what was happening... I just felt completely flat.\n\nDoctor: When the paramedics were working on your husband in the kitchen, did you feel like your own body or your surroundings seemed unreal or strange to you—like things weren't quite real, even though you knew they were happening?\n\nPatient: Well, it didn't feel like that right when the paramedics were there... but lately, yes, things have felt strange. Sometimes I'll just be walking through my own kitchen, and it looks... different. Like it's a room in someone else's house. It feels like I'm moving around in a dream, like I'm not really there. It's just a very odd feeling.\n\nDoctor: When exactly did you first start noticing that things felt unreal or dream-like, and that your kitchen looked like someone else's house? Was that feeling there right from the moment the paramedics arrived, or did it develop gradually over the days and weeks after your husband's death?\n\nPatient: Well... it's been going on for about two weeks. Basically ever since my husband collapsed and the paramedics worked on him in our kitchen. Like I said, I didn't feel it the exact minute they were in there, but it started right after that day. So it's been about two weeks now of things just... feeling strange like that.\n\nDoctor: When you're going about your day—like making breakfast or watching television—do you feel that dream-like, unreal sensation most of the time, or does it mainly happen when you're in the kitchen or thinking about what happened to your husband?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. Um... I haven't really kept track of exactly when it happens. I know it happens in the kitchen, like I mentioned, but I couldn't tell you if it's going on most of the day or just when my mind goes back to what happened. I just haven't noticed a specific pattern to it.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something completely unrelated to what happened—like cooking dinner or talking on the phone with a friend—do you still get that dream-like, unreal feeling, or does it mainly come back when you're thinking about your husband or in the kitchen?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, I really don't know for sure. I haven't paid attention to whether I'm on the phone or doing something else when it happens. I'm sorry, I just haven't kept track of it that closely. It just sort of comes over me sometimes, but I couldn't tell you exactly what I'm doing when it starts.\n\nDoctor: Right now, in this moment, sitting here talking with me in my office—do you feel that same dream-like, unreal sensation, or does that feeling mainly happen when you're at home or when your mind goes back to what happened with your husband?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel it right now. Sitting here with you, things seem... normal, I guess. It's not happening at this exact moment. Like I said before, I just haven't figured out exactly when or why it starts up.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that most of your day feels dream-like and unreal, or is it more like you have moments or episodes where it happens and then it passes?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say if it's most of the day or just in moments. I just haven't been keeping track of it like that. It happens, but I don't know how long it lasts or how much of the day it takes up. I'm sorry, I just don't have a good sense of the time when it comes to that feeling.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, before you've had time to think about what happened to your husband, do you already feel that dream-like, unreal sensation—or does it only start once you begin thinking about him or going about your day?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I've been saying, I just haven't been paying that much attention to when it starts or stops. I wake up, and... I don't know if it's there right away or if it comes on later. I'm sorry, I just really haven't noticed if it happens first thing in the morning or not.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband passed away two weeks ago, have there been any full days where you did NOT feel that dream-like, unreal sensation at all—or has it been happening every single day?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. I... I just haven't been keeping track of days like that. The last two weeks are just a blur, honestly. I don't know if there was a day where it didn't happen at all, or if it's been every day. I'm sorry, I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that has nothing to do with your husband—like eating a meal, or taking a shower, or getting dressed—do you feel that dream-like, unreal sensation during those activities, or does it mainly happen when something reminds you of what happened?\n\nPatient: It happens during regular things, too. Like... sometimes I'll just be walking through my own kitchen, and it suddenly looks like a room in someone else's house. It doesn't even feel like mine. I just feel like I'm moving around in a dream and I'm not really there. So, yes, it happens even when I'm just doing normal things around the house.\n\nDoctor: When you leave your house and go somewhere else—like to the grocery store or a friend's house—do you still get that dream-like, unreal feeling there, or does it mainly stay with you at home?\n\nPatient: I... well, I haven't really been going anywhere. Since he passed, I've mostly just stayed right here at home. I haven't been to the grocery store or... or over to anyone's house. So I really couldn't tell you if it happens there, too. I just haven't been out enough to know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
